\section*{How to use this packet}
For each source claim, compare the exact source excerpts with the expanded
PaperInterface specification. Mark up this PDF or fill the blank reviewer box in the TeX source;
return the annotated file for reconciliation into the paper-local human-review
log.

\section*{Open the interactive dashboard}
The interactive dashboard is an optional alternative to using this PDF; it
presents the same review surface rather than an additional review requirement.
After forking and cloning (or pulling) AppliedModelingLib, run the following from the
repository root. The cache command is needed only the first time in a new clone.
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
cd <your-repository-checkout>
lake exe cache get
python3 scripts/review_dashboard.py --paper GCG24UserItemFairness --serve
\end{ReviewVerbatim}
Open \url{http://127.0.0.1:8765/} in a browser and keep that terminal running
while reviewing. The dashboard records saved annotations in this paper's local
reviewer-owned review record.

value:
fun {n : ℕ} (alpha : ℝ) (v : GCG24UserItemFairness.Item n → ℝ) (t j : GCG24UserItemFairness.Item n) =>
  if ↑j < ↑t then
    GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.problem6ClosedValue alpha v t /
      GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.pairShare alpha v j
  else
    if j = t then
      1 -
        GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.problem6ClosedValue alpha v t *
          GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.problem6LeftSum alpha v t
    else 0
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

fun {n : ℕ} (beta : ℝ) (v x z : GCG24UserItemFairness.Item n → ℝ) (j : GCG24UserItemFairness.Item n) =>
  2 * beta *
      (GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.pairShare (1 / 2) v j * x j +
        (1 - GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.pairShare (1 / 2) v j) *
          x (GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.reverseItem j)) +
    (1 - 2 * beta) * z j
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

  {
    utility := fun (k : GCG24UserItemFairness.UserType 3) (j : GCG24UserItemFairness.Item n) =>
      if k = 0 then v j
      else
        if k = 1 then v (GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.reverseItem j)
        else (v j + v (GCG24UserItemFairness.OpposingTypes.reverseItem j)) / 2,
    weight := fun (k : GCG24UserItemFairness.UserType 3) =>
      if k = 0 then beta else if k = 1 then beta else 1 - 2 * beta }

\textbf{Declaration location:} paper-local
\paragraph{Lean declaration body}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
type:
{m n K : ℕ} →
  GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserTypeAssignment m K →
    GCG24UserItemFairness.UserTypeAssignment.TypeSymmetryLinearConstraintIndex m n →
      GCG24UserItemFairness.User m → GCG24UserItemFairness.Item n → ℝ

value:
fun {m n K : ℕ} (τ : GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserTypeAssignment m K)
    (c : GCG24UserItemFairness.UserTypeAssignment.TypeSymmetryLinearConstraintIndex m n)
    (u : GCG24UserItemFairness.User m) (j : GCG24UserItemFairness.Item n) =>
  if τ.toType c.1 = τ.toType c.2.1 then
    (if u = c.1 then if j = c.2.2 then 1 else 0 else 0) - if u = c.2.1 then if j = c.2.2 then 1 else 0 else 0
  else 0
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\textbf{Declaration location:} paper-local
\paragraph{Lean declaration body}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
type:
{m n K : ℕ} →
  GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserSymmetricEqualityLP m n K →
    GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserSymmetricEqualityLP.Constraint m n K →
      GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserSymmetricEqualityLP.Variable m n → ℝ

value:
fun {m n K : ℕ} (L : GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserSymmetricEqualityLP m n K)
    (a : GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserSymmetricEqualityLP.Constraint m n K)
    (a_1 : GCG24UserItemFairness.RecommendationModel.UserSymmetricEqualityLP.Variable m n) =>
  match a, a_1 with
  | Sum.inl (Sum.inl (Sum.inl j)), v => L.itemEqualityNormal j v
  | Sum.inl (Sum.inl (Sum.inr u)), Sum.inl (u', _j) => if u' = u then 1 else 0
  | Sum.inl (Sum.inl (Sum.inr _u)), Sum.inr val => 0
  | Sum.inl (Sum.inr c), Sum.inl (u, j) =>
    GCG24UserItemFairness.UserTypeAssignment.typeSymmetryLinearCoefficient L.types c u j
  | Sum.inl (Sum.inr _c), Sum.inr val => 0
  | Sum.inr (u, j), Sum.inl (u', j') => if u' = u ∧ j' = j then 1 else 0
  | Sum.inr (_u, _j), Sum.inr val => 0
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
Users, items, and utilities. There is a finite population of m users and n items. Let wij > 0 be
the utility of recommending item j to user i; this could represent a click-through rate or purchase
probability. We suppose that user-item utilities are symmetric: each of the user i and item j receives
utility wij from being recommended item j.
Let ∆n−1 denote the simplex in Rn . The platform’s task is to choose a recommendation policy
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

can be captured by a single parameter. Consider the following utility matrix structure. Let v1 >

\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
Section 4, suppose that there are 2 types of users, with opposing preferences (i.e., with values
v1 , v2 ..., vn and vn , vn−1 , ..., v1 , respectively) – and the platform has correctly estimated these
preferences. However, now, these two types only make up a proportion β of the population each.
Now, we suppose that there is a fraction 1 − β of the user population who are “new” users. We assume
that these users are drawn from the same distribution as the remaining users, but the platform does
not know their preferences. It thus constructs a prior by averaging over the known users’ preferences
v +v
– (mis)estimating the users’ utility for each item j as j n−j+1
.
2
6


[Next verbatim source excerpt]

The idea follows the above intuition: without item fairness constraints and assuming cold start users
follow the same distribution as existing users, the platform’s price of misestimation is low because
the platform treats cold start users as the average of the existing population. Fairness constraints,
however, can make this cost arbitrarily high, as in expectation cold start users relatively enjoy items
other users do not. Such effects suggest that a more careful treatment of uncertainty and fairness
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\paragraph{Verbatim source input}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
Proposition 1. Suppose that for a set of recommendation policies S ⊆ ∆m
n−1 ,
(i) S can be described by a finite set of linear constraints
(ii) There exists an optimal solution ρ∗ to Problem (2) such that ρ∗ ∈ S
(iii) ρ∗ is the unique feasible solution to Problem (2) in S
∗
Multiple policies ρ̂ may optimize Umin
(γ, ŵ), and different policies ρ̂ may have different values of
Umin (ρ̂, w). In our experimental results, we use the policy ρ̂ found by the convex optimization solver.
1

4


[form-feed in source extraction]
Then, finding an optimal solution ρ∗ to Problem (2) can be reduced to solving a linear program L.
With Proposition 1, the key technical challenges become: (a) given utility matrix w, finding S
satisfying the above conditions; (b) given S and w, finding a closed-form expression for ρ∗ ; (c) given
∗
a closed form for ρ∗ in terms of w, reasoning about the properties of item fair solution Umin
(1, w).
To do this, for our main results, we construct S as a set of recommendations with a particular
symmetric structure. Suppose users come in K types, where a user of type k shares a utility vector.
Then, consider Ssymm = {ρ : ρi = ρi′ if wi = wi′ } ⊆ ∆m
n−1 , the set of policies where users of the
same type are given the same recommendation probabilities. Note that, in the extreme case, each user
is their own type. Furthermore, let ρk denote the recommendation policy for type k, for ρ ∈ Ssymm .
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\section*{Review row 2}
\textbf{Source locator:} {\footnotesize\raggedright cited publication, lines 312-\allowbreak{}338\par}
\paragraph{Verbatim source input}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
symmetric structure. Suppose users come in K types, where a user of type k shares a utility vector.
Then, consider Ssymm = {ρ : ρi = ρi′ if wi = wi′ } ⊆ ∆m
n−1 , the set of policies where users of the
same type are given the same recommendation probabilities. Note that, in the extreme case, each user
is their own type. Furthermore, let ρk denote the recommendation policy for type k, for ρ ∈ Ssymm .
Proposition 2. Ssymm satisfies conditions (i) and (ii) in Proposition 1. Furthermore, solutions
ρ ∈ Ssymm to Problem (2) have a sparse structure:
• If ρ is a basic feasible solution to the linear program L in Proposition 1, then there are at
most n + K − 1 type-item pairs (k, j) such that ρkj > 0 (out of nK possible pairs).
• If ρ is also optimal, then there are at most K − 1 items that are ever recommended to more
than one type of user, i.e., where ρkj , ρk′ j > 0 for k ̸= k ′ .
For our main results, we will leverage the sparsity structure in Proposition 2 to show, with further
restrictions on utility matrix w, that Ssymm also satisfies (iii). We will then derive closed-form
expressions for that solution, and show how it changes as a function of w.
The sparsity structure in Proposition 2 is also interesting in its own right. Without item fairness
constraints, solutions will be highly sparse: each user will be recommended their most preferred item
deterministically; each other item will have a recommendation probability of zero. Once we add item
fairness constraints, solutions do not necessarily remain sparse. However, Proposition 2 shows that
sparse solutions still arise under item fairness constraints in settings with symmetric solutions.

4

User preference diversity and fairness tradeoffs

We now use the theoretical framework developed in the above section to understand the effect of the
structure of user utilities on the price of fairness. In particular, we identify “free fairness,” i.e., the
price of fairness is low, when preferences are sufficiently diverse.

[form-feed in source extraction]
We now formalize and generalize this example, when the level of heterogeneity in the population
can be captured by a single parameter. Consider the following utility matrix structure. Let v1 >
v2 > ... > vn > 0. Suppose that there are two user types: a user i either has utility wij = vj or
wij = vn−j+1 , and say that user i is of type 1 or 2 respectively. In words, the two types have opposite
preferences, but the preferences can otherwise be generic and be for any number of items. Now, the
direct solution by symmetry for the example no longer directly holds – the items in the middle, not
necessarily preferred by either user type, may be binding in terms of item fairness constraints.
Let α be the proportion of type 1 users in the population, out of a fixed population of m users.

[Next verbatim source excerpt]

We now formalize the above argument, building on the analysis in the previous section. As in
Section 4, suppose that there are 2 types of users, with opposing preferences (i.e., with values
v1 , v2 ..., vn and vn , vn−1 , ..., v1 , respectively) – and the platform has correctly estimated these
preferences. However, now, these two types only make up a proportion β of the population each.
Now, we suppose that there is a fraction 1 − β of the user population who are “new” users. We assume
that these users are drawn from the same distribution as the remaining users, but the platform does
not know their preferences. It thus constructs a prior by averaging over the known users’ preferences
v +v
– (mis)estimating the users’ utility for each item j as j n−j+1
.

[form-feed in source extraction]
We now formalize and generalize this example, when the level of heterogeneity in the population
can be captured by a single parameter. Consider the following utility matrix structure. Let v1 >
v2 > ... > vn > 0. Suppose that there are two user types: a user i either has utility wij = vj or
wij = vn−j+1 , and say that user i is of type 1 or 2 respectively. In words, the two types have opposite
preferences, but the preferences can otherwise be generic and be for any number of items. Now, the
direct solution by symmetry for the example no longer directly holds – the items in the middle, not
necessarily preferred by either user type, may be binding in terms of item fairness constraints.
Let α be the proportion of type 1 users in the population, out of a fixed population of m users.

[Next verbatim source excerpt]

We now formalize the above argument, building on the analysis in the previous section. As in
Section 4, suppose that there are 2 types of users, with opposing preferences (i.e., with values
v1 , v2 ..., vn and vn , vn−1 , ..., v1 , respectively) – and the platform has correctly estimated these
preferences. However, now, these two types only make up a proportion β of the population each.
Now, we suppose that there is a fraction 1 − β of the user population who are “new” users. We assume
that these users are drawn from the same distribution as the remaining users, but the platform does
not know their preferences. It thus constructs a prior by averaging over the known users’ preferences
v +v
– (mis)estimating the users’ utility for each item j as j n−j+1
.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}
